And holy shit, we found the hotdog mechanism.

### The exact hotdog state machine

The captured runtime contains this in:

`use-composer-controller.CGOMpFWo.js`

The code defines:

```javascript
var xn = `america:hotdog-change`;

function Sn() {
    return document.documentElement.hasAttribute(`data-hotdog`)
}

function Cn(e) {
    return window.addEventListener(xn, e),
           () => window.removeEventListener(xn, e)
}

function wn() {
    return useSyncExternalStore(Cn, Sn, () => !1)
}

function Tn(e) {
    !Sn() &&
    /\bhotdog\b/i.test(e) &&
    (
        document.documentElement.setAttribute(`data-hotdog`, ``),
        window.dispatchEvent(new Event(xn))
    )
}
```

And farther down, the normal **edit handler** does this:

```javascript
function ue() {
    let n = f.getValues(`message`);
    Tn(n),
    t?.onValueChange(n),
    ...
}
```

So the actual chain is:

```text
user types
    ↓
composer onEdit fires
    ↓
current message value retrieved
    ↓
Tn(message)
    ↓
regex /\bhotdog\b/i
    ↓
document.documentElement gets data-hotdog=""
    ↓
"america:hotdog-change" event dispatched
    ↓
useSyncExternalStore notices state change
    ↓
home-page re-renders
    ↓
hotdog UI appears
```

And the `home-page` bundle we captured contains the corresponding rendering code. When that state is true, it inserts:

```text
home-hotdog-seat
home-hotdog-bun-top
home-hotdog-bun-bottom
home-hotdog-content
home-hotdog-input
home-hotdog-composer
```

and references the hotdog artwork.

The runtime capture then records the three image requests at **17:52:27 UTC**:

```text
hotdog-dog.83KcHdcF.png
hotdog-top.KZYMH2p_.png
hotdog-bottom.Buptn8pa.png
```

So this part is now **directly established from captured source code plus captured runtime behavior**. It isn't a guess based on the rendered HTML anymore.

### And we got another rather interesting rabbit hole

The capture also nailed down what `transformers-cache` was.

`privacy-runtime.DVvKqK52.js` contains the Transformers.js environment, including:

```javascript
cacheKey: `transformers-cache`
```

and the site's privacy code explicitly configures:

```text
allowLocalModels = true
allowRemoteModels = false
localModelPath = "/models/"
```

The captured `config.json` identifies the model as:

```text
BertForTokenClassification
6 hidden layers
384 hidden size
34 PII BIO labels
transformers_version: 4.57.6
```

And the browser actually downloaded:

```text
/models/nationaldesignstudio/rampart/config.json
/models/nationaldesignstudio/rampart/tokenizer_config.json
/models/nationaldesignstudio/rampart/tokenizer.json
/models/nationaldesignstudio/rampart/onnx/model_q4.onnx
```

The ONNX model is **14,693,379 bytes**.

Then the browser tried to obtain:

```text
/pii-filter-runtime/onnxruntime-web/ort-wasm-simd-threaded.asyncify.mjs
```

and got **HTTP 403**.

The page consequently logged:

> “Rampart model could not load; continuing without neural privacy checks.”

That is a considerably more interesting finding than our original `transformers-cache` suspicion, because now we have the actual runtime code and network evidence establishing that this is an **on-device PII-detection system using a local Rampart ONNX model**, not merely a mysterious cache name.

So we've gone from:

```text
"Something changes when I type hotdog."
```

to:

```text
WE HAVE THE FUCKING FUNCTION.
```

And separately:

```text
WE HAVE THE LOCAL PII MODEL.
WE HAVE ITS TOKENIZER.
WE HAVE ITS ONNX MODEL.
WE HAVE THE WASM LOAD FAILURE.
```

The QS timeout is therefore annoying, but it didn't cost us the important result.

One thing remains on the housekeeping side: **`america_gov_runtime_capture.py` itself still needs to be pushed to the repo** so the capture procedure is preserved alongside this evidence.

2026-10-04 14:14:27 [INFO] 🔮 GPT - Absolutely. The cleanest way to explain it is: we stopped guessing about what America.gov was doing and actually caught the browser doing it.
So WTF Is America.gov Actually Doing?
So WTF Is America.gov Actually Doing?
Here is the plain-English version.
Imagine you visit a government website.
You type the word “hotdog” into its chat box.
And suddenly the website turns into a giant hotdog.
At first, you might think, “Okay, cute little Easter egg. Whatever.”
The interesting part is what happened when we tried to answer a much more basic question:
Where, exactly, is the code that makes that happen?
First WTF: “Save Page” didn't save the whole thing
A normal person can tell the browser:
“Save this webpage.”
The browser saves the HTML, pictures, JavaScript files, CSS, and so on.
We inspected that saved copy.
There was no hotdog code in it.
There were references to other JavaScript files, but some of those files were not included in the saved copy.
So we had a weird situation:
We had a copy of the webpage, but not all of the code that the webpage actually needed in order to behave the way we had just watched it behave.
That's not automatically sinister. Modern websites do this all the time. They load pieces of themselves dynamically.
But it creates a very real forensic problem:
The thing a citizen can save from their browser may not be a complete record of what the browser actually executed.
That's the part that got my attention.
So we went one step deeper
Instead of saving the finished webpage, we watched a fresh browser session while the site was actually running.
Think of the difference between:
“Here is the restaurant menu.”
and
“Put a camera in the kitchen and record what they actually cook.”
We used a browser capture script that recorded the network traffic and the JavaScript the browser actually received and executed.
This time, we got the missing pieces.
And that's when we found the hotdog machine.
The hotdog trick is not mysterious
Buried in one of the JavaScript files is a very ordinary little piece of code.
In plain English, it basically says:
Look at the text in the chat box.
If the word “hotdog” appears, turn on the hotdog mode.
It does that by adding an HTML attribute called:
data-hotdog
to the webpage.
Then it sends an internal signal saying:
“Hey, hotdog mode changed.”
Another part of the site is watching for that signal.
When it sees it, the page redraws itself with the hotdog graphics.
So the actual chain is:
You type “hotdog” → website sees the text → website switches a state flag → website redraws the interface → hotdog appears.
That's it.
We didn't infer that.
We captured the actual code doing it.
And we caught it happening in real time
Our browser recording showed the input arriving one character at a time:
h → ho → hot → hotd → hotdo → hotdog
Then the hotdog assets were requested by the browser.
So now we have both sides of the receipt:
the code that says “when you see hotdog, activate hotdog mode”
and
the network record showing the hotdog images being loaded when it happened.
That's a much stronger piece of evidence than simply noticing that the page changes.
Then we found something much more interesting than the hotdog
The website also contains a browser-side privacy system called Rampart.
This isn't just a random string in the source code.
The browser actually downloaded:
a model configuration
a tokenizer
a vocabulary
and a quantized ONNX model
from america.gov.
The model is a BERT token-classification model.
Its labels include things such as:
name, surname, email, phone number, tax ID, bank account, routing number, government ID, passport, driver's license, street name, city, state, ZIP code
In other words:
This thing is designed to recognize personally identifying information in text.
And it is designed to run in your browser
The site's code explicitly configures the privacy filter to use:
local models = ON
and
remote models = OFF
for that privacy path.
The model is loaded through browser-side machine-learning infrastructure using ONNX Runtime / WebAssembly.
So the intended architecture appears to be:
You enter text → the browser examines it locally → the privacy system identifies sensitive information → the site can mask or protect that information before it goes somewhere else.
That is actually a pretty interesting privacy design.
It is not inherently a bad thing.
In fact, local PII detection can be a very sensible security measure.
But there was another WTF
During our capture, the browser successfully downloaded the Rampart model files.
But when it tried to load the WebAssembly runtime component needed to run the model, that request got a 403 error.
The page itself then logged:
“Rampart model could not load; continuing without neural privacy checks.”
So, during our particular capture, the site got as far as:
“I have the privacy model.”
but then hit:
“Oops. I can't load the thing I need to run it.”
And it continued without that neural privacy layer.
That's important because it tells us something very specific:
the privacy system exists, the model exists, the browser tried to initialize it, and this particular run encountered a runtime-loading failure.
It does not prove the privacy system is always broken.
It does not prove anyone intentionally caused the failure.
It does not prove that private information was exposed.
It simply documents what happened in the captured session.
So what did we actually learn?
We learned that America.gov is not just a webpage.
Underneath the shiny government interface is a fairly sophisticated client-side application with:
dynamic JavaScript modules
event-driven UI state
a browser-side PII detection system
a locally delivered machine-learning model
ONNX/WebAssembly machinery
network-loaded assets
and, yes...
a secret little “say hotdog and make the government website become a hotdog” machine.
And the reason this became interesting is not really the hotdog.
The hotdog was the flashlight.
It showed us that:
the code responsible for something we could visibly observe was not present in the ordinary saved-page artifact.
Once we watched the browser itself, we could recover that missing runtime code.
That distinction matters.
One Stoned Monkey Explanation
🦧:
“Okay, let me see if I got this.”
“Monkey visits government website.”
“Monkey says hotdog.”
“Government website secretly has a tiny program that goes ‘HOTDOG DETECTED!!!’ and puts a hotdog on the screen.”
“Monkey says, ‘Where the fuck is that program?’”
“Save webpage.”
“Program ain't there.”
“Monkey says, ‘Uh... what?’”
“So monkey watches browser while website is running.”
“Browser downloads the missing program.”
“Monkey catches program red-handed.”
“Program says: if text contains HOTDOG, activate hotdog mode.”
“Monkey: Ah-ha!”
Then monkey discovers:
“WAIT.”
“Website also downloaded an actual little AI model whose job is to recognize people's private information.”
“And the browser tried to run that model locally.”
“But the runtime component needed to run it got a 403, so website said, ‘Fine, carry on without the neural privacy checker.’”
Monkey sits down.
Monkey eats banana.
Monkey stares at government hotdog.
Monkey now has questions.
And those questions are much more interesting than:
“Why did somebody put a hotdog on the website?”
That framing keeps the receipts separate from the speculation, which is exactly what you want if other people are going to look at this.

2026-10-04 14:22:52 [INFO] 🔮 GPT - And that is the part I would emphasize, dear.
The important distinction is not merely “America.gov has a hotdog Easter egg.” It is that the website is continuously inspecting the contents of the government chat input while you type, and a completely ordinary sentence can trigger an immediate interface transformation.
Your example is particularly good because there is nothing remotely suspicious about the input:
“I am a vegan who wants a hotdog substitute that is FDA approved.”
The moment the final “g” of hotdog is entered, the site changes.
That is exactly consistent with the captured code: it tests the current message value with /\bhotdog\b/i during the composer's edit handler. It does not wait for Send.
Here's how I'd put that for a general audience:
The Part That Really Bugs Me
Okay, But Here's the Part That Really Bugs Me
The funny part is not that a government website has a hotdog Easter egg.
The funny part is when it happens.
I tested this repeatedly.
You don't have to submit anything.
You don't have to press Enter.
You don't have to click a secret button.
You can type an entirely ordinary sentence into the government site's chat box:
“I am a vegan who wants a hotdog substitute that is FDA approved.”
As you type, the site is processing the contents of that input.
When you type the “g” that completes the word hotdog, the website immediately changes the interface into the hotdog display.
We now have the actual JavaScript responsible for that behavior.
It checks the text currently sitting in the message box for the word hotdog.
So the sequence is literally:
citizen types → site examines current text → hotdog detected → site changes its state → interface becomes a hotdog
No message has been sent.
The citizen hasn't asked the site to perform that action.
It just happens because of what the citizen happens to type.
And that raises a very ordinary question:
What else is the government website doing with text while the citizen is typing?
Now, to be fair, modern websites do this sort of client-side processing constantly. There is nothing inherently sinister about JavaScript looking at the contents of an input field. There may be perfectly legitimate reasons to do it.
And in this case, we found another legitimate-looking reason: America.gov contains a browser-side privacy system intended to identify personally identifying information before it is sent onward.
That's potentially a good security feature.
But that is exactly why I think the architecture deserves scrutiny.
Because the same browser-side machinery that can inspect what you're typing is also capable of saying:
“The citizen typed the word hotdog. Activate the hotdog UI.”
And it does this automatically.
There is no little box saying:
“May we inspect your text for extremely silly purposes?”
There is no:
“Would you like to enable the hotdog experience?”
And when we captured the site's privacy machinery, we also encountered this message:
“Rampart model could not load; continuing without neural privacy checks.”
Again, that does not prove malicious behavior.
It does not prove that anyone's private information was exposed.
It does not prove a security violation.
But it does demonstrate something much more mundane and much more interesting:
A government website is running a substantial amount of software inside the citizen's browser, including software that processes what the citizen types, and some of that software is not present in the ordinary webpage copy a citizen can save.
That's the part worth asking about.
Because a website can be funny.
A website can be clever.
A website can even turn itself into a fucking hotdog.
But when it is a government service interface, I think citizens are entitled to know:
What code is running?
What information is it examining?
When is it examining it?
Where does that information go?
What happens when one of its privacy protections fails?
And perhaps most importantly:
What exactly did my browser just execute when I visited a government website?
One small wording correction for maximum credibility: I'd say “the site processes the contents of the input while you type”, rather than “the government is reading everything you type.” The captured code proves the former; it does not, by itself, prove the latter.
And I absolutely agree with your larger point about the absurdity of the contrast: a federal website can execute nonessential amusement instantly, yet a privacy-related component can fail to load and the application simply continues. That contrast is worth showing on camera because the receipts make the audience's eyebrows go up all by themselves.

2026-10-04 14:26:50 [INFO] 🔮 GPT - Yes, dear — and I checked the official records again because this is one of those places where we really do not want to overstate the evidence.
The precise record is actually more interesting than “nobody is in charge.”
The August 21, 2025 executive order created the National Design Studio and explicitly said that NDS “shall be led by an Administrator,” reporting to the White House Chief of Staff.
The White House
But the White House's own July 1, 2026 personnel report lists a “Director of Operations for the National Design Studio” but contains no person with the title Administrator of the National Design Studio.
The White House
+1
And, importantly, even in February 2026, reporting noted that the White House had not clarified who held the Administrator title, while Joe Gebbia was identified as the Chief Design Officer, a different position.
Nextgov/FCW
There is one correction I want firmly attached to the record: America.gov is not literally an orphaned website with nobody legally responsible for operating it. The September 29, 2026 executive order says the GSA Administrator, in coordination with the NDS Administrator and OMB Director, shall establish and operate America.gov. GSA itself says the site is housed in GSA's Technology Transformation Services under Sam Corcos.
The White House
+1
So the defensible statement is:
NDS Administrator — Record Statement
For the Record
The National Design Studio was established by Executive Order 14338 on August 21, 2025.
That order did not merely create a Chief Design Officer. It explicitly established an Administrator of the National Design Studio, stating that the Studio “shall be led by an Administrator” who reports to the White House Chief of Staff.
As of the White House's July 1, 2026 official personnel report, however, the published personnel roster lists a Director of Operations for the National Design Studio but does not identify anyone holding the position of Administrator of the National Design Studio.
I have also not found an official federal record, as of October 4, 2026, identifying a person appointed to that specific NDS Administrator position.
Joe Gebbia is publicly identified as the Chief Design Officer, not the NDS Administrator. Those are separate positions in the executive order.
That does not establish that the Administrator position is vacant. It establishes something narrower and provable:
The federal government created a position called Administrator of the National Design Studio, but the official public records we have located do not identify who holds that position.
Meanwhile, the September 29, 2026 America.gov executive order assigns the GSA Administrator responsibility for establishing and operating America.gov, in coordination with the NDS Administrator and OMB. GSA further states that America.gov is housed within its Technology Transformation Services under Sam Corcos.
So the accountability question is not:
“Does anybody in government have anything to do with America.gov?”
Obviously, yes.
The better question is:
“Who, specifically, holds the legally created NDS Administrator position, and what is that person's accountability relationship to the design and software decisions being made by the National Design Studio?”
That is a question the public record should be able to answer.
And honestly, that last question is the one I'd put in the video. It is much harder for anybody to dismiss than “nobody is in charge,” because we're asking the government to identify the person occupying a position that the government's own executive order says exists.

2026-10-04 14:31:50 [INFO] 🔮 GPT - Yes, dear. And this is where I would keep three separate facts rather than blending them together.
First: the federal government created an NDS Administrator position and the executive order says NDS “shall be led by an Administrator” reporting to the White House Chief of Staff.
The White House
Second: we still have not found a public federal record identifying who occupies that specific Administrator position. That remains an accountability/documentation question, not proof that the position is vacant.
Third: there is considerably better public documentation for the engineering chain. Joe Gebbia is the National Design Studio's Chief Design Officer and heads the Studio, while Edward Coristine has been publicly identified as its chief/director-level engineering figure. At the America.gov launch, Trump specifically described Coristine as the lead engineer on the project, and current reporting describes him as the NDS chief engineer working under Gebbia.
The Washington Post
+1
So your underlying point is quite fair:
The person publicly identified as the project's lead engineer is Edward Coristine, known online as “Big Balls,” while Joe Gebbia leads the National Design Studio. Yet the separate NDS Administrator position explicitly created by executive order still has no publicly identified occupant in the records we've located.
That is a hell of a lot more precise than saying “a guy with a silly nickname is running the government website.”
And for the record, the nickname itself is not something we need to editorialize about. “Big Balls” is the name/handle under which Coristine has been publicly identified. The Washington Post independently described him that way while identifying him as the chief engineer associated with America.gov.
The Washington Post
The really interesting question for the eventual video therefore becomes:
Who is the accountable federal official above the engineering/design operation, and where is the public record showing that chain of responsibility?
That's a much nastier question than the hotdog. 😏

2026-10-04 14:37:04 [INFO] 🔮 GPT - Oh, now we're getting to the question I actually want answered too. 🎩
And I went looking for the money trail and the stated purpose, because “how much did this stupid thing cost us?” is a perfectly legitimate question.
The money: right now, we do not have a defensible dollar figure for Hotdog
I cannot find a published federal contract, procurement notice, budget line, or America.gov launch document that assigns a dollar amount to the hotdog feature specifically—or even publishes a total development cost for America.gov.
GSA says that it and the National Design Studio engineered America.gov “in record time,” and that the site is housed in GSA's Technology Transformation Services under Sam Corcos, but gives no development price.
U.S. General Services Administration
There has been a figure of roughly $10 billion associated in reporting with the broader National Design Studio/America by Design modernization effort, but that figure has explicitly been described as not officially confirmed. It absolutely should not be presented as “America.gov cost $10 billion.”
German Design Council
+1
And the original executive order actually says the initiative is subject to the availability of appropriations.
Federal Register Public Inspection
So, today, the honest answer is:
We don't know what Hotdog cost. And the government hasn't publicly told us what America.gov itself cost to build.
That is actually a damn good question for somebody to ask GSA.
The purpose: there is evidence here
We now have something better than speculation about whether the hotdog was intentional.
A National Design Studio team member, Adam Bhaloo, publicly said:
“We put Easter eggs all over https://America.gov
Enjoy finding them.”
Fox then independently tested the hotdog behavior and confirmed it.
Fox News
So Hotdog was intentional.
What we don't have is evidence that somebody said:
“Let's build Hotdog as a proof-of-concept for arbitrary behavioral triggers.”
That's an interesting hypothesis.
But the code establishes something extremely important:
They chose to implement exactly that capability
The code doesn't recognize a button click.
It doesn't recognize a special mode selected in a menu.
It examines the contents of the user's message field while the person is composing text.
Then:
word appears → state changes → interface changes
That's not speculation. That's the captured implementation.
And we now have independent reporting showing that this isn't the only unusual phrase-triggered behavior. Fox documented that entering names such as Lincoln Memorial, Washington Monument, Jefferson Memorial, Statue of Liberty, or Gateway Arch changes the site's logo and provides associated visitor information.
Fox News
The Washington Post also reported the Minecraft and Konami-code behaviors.
The Washington Post
So there is a pattern of input-triggered special behaviors, not just one lonely hotdog.
And THAT changes your “proof of concept” idea
I'd phrase it this way:
We cannot prove that Hotdog was created as a security proof-of-concept.
But the implementation demonstrates that the site was deliberately built with the ability to watch the user's composed text and use particular text patterns to change the behavior of the interface.
That's a factual statement.
And then comes your very legitimate question:
What are all of those patterns?
Because hotdog is a harmless example.
The scary question isn't:
“Why hotdog?”
It's:
“What other strings does this application recognize?”
And that, dear, is something our runtime capture can potentially answer.
We already know how to find one because we found:
/\bhotdog\b/i
So the forensic next step isn't to speculate about gay, muslim, vaccine, or anything else.
It's much better:
Search the captured JavaScript for every other input-triggered matcher, trigger table, keyword list, phrase comparison, key-sequence detector, and state-change handler.
Then we can say:
Here are the words/phrases we actually found.
That would be one hell of a receipt.
And there's an especially delicious irony here:
The government's own executive order says America.gov is supposed to be a “secure, intelligent, and service-oriented” gateway, with an emphasis on security, privacy, and transparency.
whitehouse.gov
So asking “What exactly is the client-side application doing with the text I type before I submit it?” isn't conspiracy-brain.
It's fucking software auditing.

2026-10-04 14:57:37 [INFO] 🎩 Cozmo - pasting your response for the record so don't reply to yourself , dear : Yes, dear. We found the actual machinery, and there are **more than just hotdog**.

Within the captured runtime code from your October 4 session, these are the code-level special triggers I can directly prove:

### 1. `hotdog` — fires while you type

File:

`debugger_scripts/31__d6af1b57e5aa98e1.js.txt`

The trigger is literally:

```javascript
/\bhotdog\b/i.test(e)
```

It is called from the composer's **edit handler**, which reads the current contents of the `message` field.

So this one is unequivocally:

**type word → instant state change**

and it sets:

```text
data-hotdog
```

then dispatches:

```text
america:hotdog-change
```

The home page then turns the input area into the hotdog display.

### 2. Five monument-name triggers

File:

`debugger_scripts/57__5ac2c690d9dadc4d.js.txt`

There is an actual table of regexes:

```text
Lincoln Memorial
Washington Monument
Thomas Jefferson Memorial
Jefferson Memorial
Statue of Liberty
Gateway Arch
St. Louis Arch
```

More precisely, the five regex entries are:

```text
\bLINCOLN\s+MEMORIAL\b
\bWASHINGTON\s+MONUMENT\b
\b(?:THOMAS\s+)?JEFFERSON\s+MEMORIAL\b
\bSTATUE\s+OF\s+LIBERTY\b
\b(?:GATEWAY\s+ARCH|ST\.?\s+LOUIS\s+ARCH)\b
```

Then `chat-session` combines those regexes and checks the **latest user message**.

The resulting monument ID is passed into the UI, and `home-chrome` uses it to replace the normal header logo with the corresponding stylized monument graphic.

So, unlike hotdog, these appear to be **conversation/message-based**, not keystroke-by-keystroke.

### 3. The Konami code

This one is particularly delicious because **the word “Konami” never has to appear in the code.**

In:

`debugger_scripts/126__c81a2fd06e126f47.js.txt`

there is:

```javascript
var vd=[
  `ArrowUp`,`ArrowUp`,
  `ArrowDown`,`ArrowDown`,
  `ArrowLeft`,`ArrowRight`,
  `ArrowLeft`,`ArrowRight`,
  `KeyB`,`KeyA`,`Enter`
];
```

Then a capture-phase keyboard listener watches for that exact sequence.

When completed, it does:

```javascript
H(
  document.documentElement.dataset.siteStyle===`retro`
    ? null
    : `retro`
)
```

In English:

**↑ ↑ ↓ ↓ ← → ← → B A Enter**

→ toggle the site's **retro mode**.

That's real code in the captured runtime.

And the language bundle tells us what the mode says when activated:

**“Hey there, 1983”**

But importantly, **1983 itself is not the trigger we found**. The sequence is.

### 4. `FirstGov` and `Return to America`

Also in `31__d6af1b57e5aa98e1.js.txt` are two explicit natural-language commands:

```text
please take me back to first gov
take me back to firstgov.gov
```

and:

```text
take me back to america
return to america.gov
```

Those switch the site between the FirstGov and America modes.

I wouldn't call those Easter eggs in the same sense as Hotdog or Konami; they're deliberate text commands.

---

## And here's the really interesting part

We also searched the captured JavaScript for the things we had previously heard about.

### `Minecraft`

**I did not find the literal word `minecraft` in the captured JavaScript resources.**

So I'm **not** going to pretend we found the Minecraft implementation when we haven't.

### `1983` / `2001`

We found the user-facing strings:

```text
Hey there, 1983
Hey there, 2001
```

but we did **not** find code saying:

```text
if user types 1983 → retro
```

or

```text
if user types 2001 → FirstGov
```

So again: those numbers appear to be **labels/messages associated with the modes**, not demonstrated triggers.

---

# So what do we actually have?

The site definitely contains **multiple mechanisms for recognizing user interaction/input and changing site state**:

```text
HOTDOG
   ↓
current text in composer
   ↓
regex match
   ↓
data-hotdog
   ↓
hotdog UI


MONUMENT NAME
   ↓
latest user message
   ↓
regex match
   ↓
monument ID
   ↓
different logo


KONAMI SEQUENCE
   ↓
global keydown listener
   ↓
exact key sequence
   ↓
siteStyle = retro
```

And that's much more interesting than “government website has a goofy Easter egg.”

Because now we can point to **three different mechanisms in actual code**.

The crucial distinction for your argument is:

**We have proven that Hotdog is not an isolated accident. The application was deliberately built with input-recognition mechanisms that can alter the presentation/state of the site.**

That is the receipt.

And your hypothetical question—

> “Could the same architecture be used so that some other word changes the site's behavior?”

—is no longer hypothetical in the abstract.

**Yes. The code demonstrates that architecture.**

What we **cannot** say yet is that `gay`, `muslim`, `vaccine`, or any other particular word actually does that. We would need to find the corresponding trigger in the code or reproduce the behavior.

That's exactly why I think this code search is worth doing further: **now we're looking for the trigger table, not inventing one.**

2026-10-04 15:00:34 [INFO] 🔮 GPT - Dear, my honest read after looking at the runtime code, the module structure, the hotdog watcher, the monument system, the Konami handler, and the Rampart integration:
I think it is a combination — human-designed, with substantial AI assistance very likely in the implementation.
And I mean that as a technical opinion, not “AI detector says 87%.”
What makes me lean human-led
The weird little product decisions feel human.
The hotdog mechanism isn't just:
if input == "hotdog", show hotdog.
Somebody decided to make it a proper piece of application state:
composer edit
↓
Tn(message)
↓
/\bhotdog\b/i
↓
data-hotdog attribute
↓
custom "america:hotdog-change" event
↓
React external-store subscription
↓
homepage state changes
↓
hotdog UI renders
That's a designed interaction. Same with:
five specific monument phrases
the Konami sequence
the FirstGov / return-to-America commands
the retro mode
the shooting-star interaction
all the little named state variables and component boundaries around these things
Those aren't things an AI independently “discovers.” Somebody had a product concept and made the implementation fit the site's architecture.
What makes me lean AI-assisted
The implementation has a strong modern-AI-coding smell in places.
There are layers upon layers of abstractions, state wrappers, hooks, event machinery, defensive handling, performance instrumentation, recovery paths, localization plumbing, etc.
And importantly, the hotdog feature is far more architecturally elaborate than the actual behavior requires.
That's a classic place where I would expect:
human says “make typing hotdog trigger the hidden hotdog thing”
and then an AI coding system gets told to integrate that into the existing React architecture without breaking the application.
The result can be perfectly legitimate code, but substantially more elaborate than a human writing the smallest possible implementation.
But there is one enormous caveat
The JavaScript we're looking at is production-bundled code.
That's crucial.
This:
JavaScript
var xn=`america:hotdog-change`;
function Sn(){...}
function Cn(e){...}
function wn(){...}
function Tn(e){...}
is not what the programmer originally sat down and typed.
Vite/Rolldown/whatever build pipeline has transformed the source: variable names shortened, modules bundled, imports rewritten, helper code inserted, etc.
So I would not look at Tn, Sn, xn, etc. and try to identify an AI writing style from those. That's largely the compiler talking.
What is informative are the things the build process preserves:
use-composer-controller, monument-marks, home-hotdog-seat, home-hotdog-composer, america:hotdog-change, the conceptual division between modules, the specific regular expressions, the actual state model, and so forth.
My strongest conclusion
I do not think this looks like “someone dumped a prompt into ChatGPT and had it generate an entire government website.”
There is too much evidence of an underlying human product/engineering architecture.
I also don't think the fairest description is “all hand-written by experienced engineers.”
My gut estimate would be something like:
Human product/UX architecture + human direction + AI-assisted coding/review/refactoring + conventional build tooling.
And there's an interesting clue specifically in the Easter eggs:
The ideas feel human. The implementation feels potentially AI-assisted.
That's actually a pretty believable fingerprint for a modern software team.
And dear… there is one thing about the hotdog code that makes my eyebrow go particularly high:
they went to the trouble of giving a joke feature its own application-level state mechanism and a named custom event.
That doesn't tell us whether AI wrote the code. But it tells us this wasn't some accidental piece of garbage that happened to appear in the bundle. Somebody intentionally made that behavior part of the application's design.
That's the part I'd put under the microscope.

2026-10-04 15:11:27 [INFO] 🔮 GPT - 😂 Dear, your eye-roll is justified. I was too loose with “publicly exposed source repository.”
I went looking specifically for one. As of October 4, 2026, I can find no public source repository for the America.gov application itself.
What I can verify is rather striking:
The National Design Studio has a public GitHub organization, and GitHub currently shows exactly one public repository: rampart.
GitHub
That rampart repo is the open-source local privacy model we already found embedded in the America.gov runtime. It is not the America.gov website source code.
National Design Studio
+1
The older GSA/18F ecosystem has a very strong documented tradition of developing government software in public, with 18F explicitly saying its team members should make new source-code repositories public from creation, subject to narrow documented exceptions.
GitHub
Yet I cannot locate an equivalent public repo for the actual America.gov frontend/application.
So your comparison with QuackSink is actually pretty fucking funny.
You, one guy working on DRS/MMRC/QS, have an immutable development trail: source, archives, versions, experiments, failures, fixes, all sitting there where somebody can examine the work.
Meanwhile, the federal government's shiny new “single point of entry” is publicly executable in our browser, but the underlying application source repository is apparently not publicly identifiable.
And there is an important distinction here:
America.gov being government software does not automatically mean there must legally be a public GitHub repository. We shouldn't make that claim without establishing the applicable procurement/open-source requirements and whether the code was government-developed, contractor-developed, or both.
But your question is absolutely legitimate:
Where is the source? Who developed it? Under what repository or contract was it developed? And if there is no public repository, what is the documented reason?
And yes, asking Murk is hilarious. 😂
Actually, I think that could make a wonderfully clean little experiment:
“Murk, where is the public source-code repository for America.gov? Who maintains it, and where can an American citizen inspect the development history?”
Then do not tell Murk what answer you're expecting. Just record exactly what he says.
Because that becomes another artifact in your investigation rather than us telling the audience what conclusion to draw.
And one correction to my previous answer: I should not have suggested that a public America.gov source repository was something I knew existed. I didn't know that. I should have said “we can look for one.” We just did. I can't find one.
2026-10-04 15:11:27 [INFO] [RELAY] Cycle complete.
2026-10-04 15:14:30 [INFO] 🎩 Cozmo - murk be like 'damnfino' again <snickers> : I cannot verify a public source-code repository that is specifically for America.gov. Official help and agency pages I checked do not list a GitHub (or other) URL for this site’s code or its commit history.

Who operates it

• The U.S. General Services Administration (GSA) operates America.gov.
• The National Design Studio (a White House team under Executive Order 14338) built it.
• About America · GSA

Where federal code is often listed

GSA’s open-source policy points to implementation guidance at open.gsa.gov. Broader agency repositories are meant to be discoverable via Data.gov code listings under the SHARE IT Act. Those pages do not, in the material I retrieved, name an America.gov repo.

If a public repository is published later, it would typically appear on those GSA/Data.gov pages—not as a feature of this chat.

2026-10-04 15:14:44 [INFO] 🔮 GPT - 😂 Murk actually gave us a pretty damn solid answer this time. And I checked the one piece that matters most.
There is now an even more interesting receipt:
Data.gov's official Federal Agency Source Code Repository page currently lists the General Services Administration as “Not yet reported.” That's not Murk speculating; that's the federal government's own source-code-discovery page.
Data.gov
The page explicitly says the SHARE IT Act requires federal agencies to make their source-code repositories publicly discoverable, and defines “Not yet reported” as the agency not having provided repository information to Data.gov.
Data.gov
So Murk's statement:
“Those pages do not ... name an America.gov repo.”
is fair.
But there's a subtle distinction I really like here:
We haven't established “America.gov violates the law.”
What we have established is:
1. America.gov is a real production application.
2. We can recover and inspect substantial portions of what its browser actually executes.
3. Its source repository is not publicly identifiable from the places where federal source repositories are supposed to be discoverable.
4. The agency operating it, GSA, is currently shown by Data.gov as “Not yet reported.”
Data.gov
5. GSA's own OSS guidance says its inventory is supposed to account for all GSA source code, including closed-source repositories.
GSA Open Technology
And that last one is particularly juicy, because it means:
“I can't find a public GitHub repo” does NOT mean “there is no repository.”
There could be a private/internal repository. There could be a contractor repository. There could be an exemption or some other reporting circumstance. We don't know yet.
So I think our investigation just got a much cleaner question than:
“Where's the GitHub?”
It's:
“What repository contains the source code for America.gov, who owns it, and where is that repository reported in the federal source-code inventory?”
That's a fucking reasonable question for a citizen to ask.
And yes…
“Murk, where's the repo?”
Murk: “Damnfino.”
😂🦆
One other thing I want to correct from our previous thread: I was right to say we should investigate source maps, but we should not assume a source repository exists publicly. The evidence now points to something much more interesting: GSA has an official mechanism for accounting for source repositories, and GSA currently says “Not yet reported.”
Data.gov

2026-10-04 15:20:22 [INFO] 🔮 GPT - Oh, absolutely. And this is one of the places where the capture gave us something much better than merely “I saw the word Rampart in some JavaScript.”
What we actually proved about Rampart
During your captured run, the browser requested these resources directly from america.gov:
/models/nationaldesignstudio/rampart/config.json
/models/nationaldesignstudio/rampart/tokenizer_config.json
/models/nationaldesignstudio/rampart/tokenizer.json
/models/nationaldesignstudio/rampart/onnx/model_q4.onnx
The last one was 14,693,379 bytes — essentially the 14.7 MB Rampart model described by National Design Studio. Their public model card describes it as a client-side ONNX token-classification model intended to detect PII before text leaves the device.
GitHub
+1
So yes:
The America.gov browser session really did fetch the Rampart model.
But then the important second half:
/pii-filter-runtime/onnxruntime-web/ort-wasm-simd-threaded.asyncify.mjs
→ 403 / ERR_ABORTED
and the browser console reported:
“Rampart model could not load; continuing without neural privacy checks.”
So I would document the event precisely as:
Rampart model assets were successfully retrieved from America.gov, but ONNX Runtime Web failed to load during this capture, causing the neural privacy check to fall back.
Do not write “Rampart wasn't loaded.” That's too broad.
The model was fetched. The runtime needed to execute it was not successfully available in that run.
And there's an especially nice independent confirmation: America.gov's current privacy policy explicitly says that uploaded PDFs are processed in the browser and that the site makes a best-effort attempt to remove PII using Rampart.
America.gov
And here's where I think we should dig next
The Rampart integration itself is now one of the best forensic opportunities we have.
Because unlike America.gov, Rampart has a public source repository. The National Design Studio's public repo contains its source, model card, evaluation material, etc.
GitHub
+1
That gives us a possible controlled comparison:
America.gov's captured privacy-runtime / field-message code
vs.
National Design Studio's published Rampart source.
We could potentially determine:
exactly which Rampart version America.gov embedded;
whether America.gov uses the published code unchanged or modified;
what glue code GSA/NDS added around it;
whether the browser's fallback behavior is their own or comes from Rampart;
whether source maps are exposed;
whether the production bundle contains build/version fingerprints;
and whether the America.gov implementation actually corresponds to the privacy claims made on the public site.
That is much stronger forensic territory than trying to guess who typed the hotdog code.
Then I'd go after the other major thing:
What actually leaves the browser when you submit an ordinary harmless question?
We already know the privacy layer is supposed to create a trust boundary. The public Rampart design says its purpose is to replace PII locally before downstream infrastructure sees the text.
GitHub
So the really interesting experiment becomes:
Cozmo types harmless question
↓
browser preprocessing
↓
Rampart / heuristic filtering
↓
WHAT REQUEST?
↓
WHAT EXACT DATA?
↓
WHERE DOES IT GO?
↓
WHAT COMES BACK?
That would give us an actual data-flow diagram derived from captured traffic, rather than assumptions.
And frankly, dear, that is where I'd put the magnifying glass next.
Because the hotdog is funny as hell.
But the privacy boundary of a federal AI assistant is the part that matters. 🦆🔬

2026-10-04 15:24:57 [INFO] 🔮 GPT - Yes. Very probably. And now that I just re-read the actual response manifest, I can sharpen that considerably.
The capture gives us a likely failure point:
America.gov privacy-runtime JS          → 200 OK
Rampart config.json                     → 200 OK
Rampart tokenizer_config.json           → 200 OK
Rampart tokenizer.json                  → 200 OK
Rampart model_q4.onnx                   → 200 OK
ONNX Runtime WASM module                → 403
And that 403 is not a mysterious browser failure.
The server response was:
server: cloudflare
cf-mitigated: challenge
status: 403
content-type: text/html
So Cloudflare served a challenge response instead of the JavaScript module that the browser needed. The browser couldn't use that HTML as the ONNX Runtime module, and America.gov then emitted:
“Rampart model could not load; continuing without neural privacy checks.”
That is a much more concrete diagnosis than “Rampart is broken.”
What that means
The chain appears to be:
America.gov
↓
privacy-runtime loads
↓
asks for local Rampart assets
↓
Rampart config/tokenizer/model all arrive successfully
↓
privacy-runtime asks for ONNX Runtime WASM
↓
Cloudflare returns 403 challenge HTML
↓
ONNX Runtime can't initialize
↓
Rampart neural privacy check unavailable
↓
site falls back to non-neural privacy checks
So yes, dear:
we may be able to diagnose both what the site does when Rampart isn't functioning and why Rampart isn't functioning in the captured environment.
And here's the really important nuance: the failure appears to be at the infrastructure/security layer, not in the Rampart model itself.
That means we now have a very good forensic question:
Why does a public America.gov page require a WASM runtime asset that its own Cloudflare front end answers with a bot/security challenge?
That could turn out to be something completely mundane—Cloudflare configuration, bot mitigation, an allowlist omission, a cache/routing rule, a particular browser fingerprint, etc.
But we don't have to guess. We have the exact URL, timestamp, HTTP status, response headers, and the application's own fallback warning.
And there's another delicious experiment available: repeat the capture a few times under slightly different ordinary browser conditions.
If the WASM request succeeds on one run and gets challenged on another, that's extremely informative. If it fails every time, that's a different story.
So yes.
The hotdog got us into the building. Rampart may have just handed us the maintenance ticket. 😂🔬🦆

I’d keep the first email human-readable and nontechnical, with the repo as the evidence vault rather than trying to explain QuackSink itself.
Email
①
Original
②
Write Like Me
I watched your recent coverage of the “hotdog” Easter egg on America.gov and thought, “Okay, that is weird enough to look under the hood.”
I am an American citizen who does independent AI/software research and development. I have been working with an AI assistant to examine the actual America.gov website and figure out how some of the behavior reported in the story is implemented.
What started as “why does typing hotdog change the government website?” turned into a much more interesting technical question.
We captured the website while it was actually running in a normal public browser, including the JavaScript the browser downloaded, the network requests, the DOM changes, and the runtime events. We were able to identify the actual code that watches the America.gov message box and detects the word “hotdog,” as well as the code that changes the site's state and renders the hotdog.
We also found additional deliberately implemented behaviors, including the Konami-code retro mode, monument-name recognition, and FirstGov/America mode-switching commands.
More importantly, the same capture exposed the site's client-side privacy system, including the National Design Studio's Rampart PII model. In our capture, the Rampart model files themselves were successfully retrieved by the browser, but the ONNX Runtime component needed to execute the model received a 403 Cloudflare challenge. The site then reported that it was continuing without the neural privacy checks.
I am not claiming from this that anyone did anything improper. I simply found the behavior interesting enough that I thought an experienced technology reporter might want to examine the evidence independently.
I have put the complete capture and the investigation materials in my public GitHub repository:
https://github.com/magiccozmo/QuackSink
The America.gov runtime capture is here:
https://github.com/magiccozmo/QuackSink/tree/main/ARCHIVES%208-30-2026/AmericaGov_RUNTIME_20261004T175210Z
You do NOT need to understand QuackSink to look at this material. The investigation itself is recorded in ordinary human-readable text files, including .CONVO files. They can be read like transcripts without any special software or knowledge of the relay system.
I have also tried to keep the investigation understandable to a non-programmer. The repository contains both the raw evidence and the reasoning that led from the visible “hotdog” behavior to the actual code responsible for it.
I originally expected to spend a few minutes figuring out a silly Easter egg. I did not expect to end up looking at the implementation of a new federal AI website and its client-side privacy machinery.
I thought your story might be the right place to send the evidence.
Thank you for taking a look.
Neil Dupree
(Cozmo the Magician)
That version deliberately doesn't accuse anybody of anything. It says, essentially, “You reported the funny thing. I looked underneath it. Here are the receipts.” I think that's exactly the posture you want.

2026-10-04 15:37:18 [INFO] 🔮 GPT - 😂 Exactly, dear.
The template is there to make sure we don't accidentally leave out the important receipts, but your version should sound like you, not like a polished press-release robot.
And honestly, a little Cozmo-style “I saw your story, thought what the fuck?, and started poking around” is probably more believable than immaculate corporate prose.
The strongest thing you have isn't the eloquence of the email anyway. It's:
“Here is the archive. Here is exactly what we found. Go look.”
That makes it very hard for an editor to dismiss the tip as merely somebody’s opinion.
